`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**The key constraint is our hardware**: The Strix Halo has 128GB LPDDR5X at ~256 GB/s — plenty of capacity but limited bandwidth for LLM inference. The critical metric is **tokens/sec on CPU**, which is purely bandwidth-bound. Smaller models = faster inference, but we need enough capability to produce valid structured JSON and understand GPU kernel optimization strategies.

**Ollama structured outputs** (v0.5+) is a game-changer we're not using yet — it forces the model to conform to a JSON schema, eliminating our manual JSON parsing + fallback logic. This would dramatically improve reliability.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Here's the analysis for our use case:

## Model Options for K-Search World Model

| Model | Size | Est. tok/s (CPU) | JSON Quality | Code Understanding | Best For |
|-------|------|-------------------|--------------|--------------------|----|
| **Phi-4-mini (3.8B)** | 2.3GB | ~15-20 | Good with structured output | Decent | World model evolution (pi_plan) |
| **Qwen2.5-coder:7b** | 4.7GB | ~5-8 | Good | Excellent | Code synthesis (pi_code) |
| **Mistral 7B** | 4.1GB | ~8-12 | Very good | Good | General-purpose alternative |
| **TinyLlama (1.1B)** | 0.6GB | ~30-40 | Weak | Minimal | Too small for our prompts |
| **qwen3-coder:30b** | 19.5GB | ~0.06 | Excellent | Excellent | Overnight only (too slow interactive) |

### Recommendation: Split models by role

The K-Search paper uses **two separate LLM roles** — we should use different models for each:

1. **pi_plan (world model)** → **Phi-4-mini** or **qwen2.5-coder:7b** — needs to output structured JSON (`INSERT/UPDATE/PRUNE`). Doesn't need deep code understanding. Speed matters more than quality here.

2. **pi_code (code synthesis)** → **qwen2.5-coder:7b** or **qwen3-coder:30b** — needs to write actual kernel code. Quality matters more than speed.

You already have `qwen2.5-coder:7b` installed. That's probably the sweet spot — fast enough for interactive (~5-8 tok/s on CPU), good enough at code and JSON.

### Structured Output Mode

The biggest win might not be a different model but using **Ollama's structured output**. Instead of hoping the model outputs valid JSON and parsing it, we can force it:

```python
# Current: hope for JSON, parse manually
response = ollama.generate(prompt)
json.loads(response)  # may fail

# Better: enforce JSON schema
response = ollama.generate(prompt, format={
    "type": "object",
    "properties": {
        "insert": {"type": "array", "items": {...}},
        "update": {"type": "object"},
        "prune": {"type": "array"}
    }
})
# Guaranteed valid JSON
```

### Fine-tuning?

For this use case, fine-tuning is **not worth it** because:
- We'd need training data (successful kernel optimization trajectories) which we're still generating
- The world model prompt already contains enough context via in-context learning
- The bottleneck is inference speed, not model quality
- Fine-tuning a 7B model takes 8-24 GPU-hours and we're competing until March 30

Fine-tuning would make sense **after** the competition, once we have 100+ cycles of real K-Search data to train on.

### Quick Win: Try qwen2.5-coder:7b

Want me to update the code to use `qwen2.5-coder:7b` as default (with Ollama structured output for the world model), and try the demo again? It should be ~10x faster than the 30B model.

{

  "old_string": "def _call_ollama(prompt: str, model: str = OLLAMA_MODEL) -> str | None:\n    \"\"\"Call local Ollama API with streaming. Returns response text or None on failure.\n\n    Uses streaming mode to avoid idle-socket timeouts on slow CPU inference.\n    Each streamed chunk resets the socket timer.\n    \"\"\"\n    payload = json.dumps({\n        \"model\": model,\n        \"prompt\": prompt,\n        \"stream\": True,\n        \"options\": {\n            \"num_predict\": MAX_TOKENS,\n            \"temperature\": 0.7,\n        },\n    }).encode()",
  "new_string": "def _call_ollama(\n    prompt: str,\n    model: str = OLLAMA_MODEL,\n    json_schema: dict | None = None,\n) -> str | None:\n    \"\"\"Call local Ollama API with streaming. Returns response text or None on failure.\n\n    Uses streaming mode to avoid idle-socket timeouts on slow CPU inference.\n    Each streamed chunk resets the socket timer.\n\n    Args:\n        json_schema: If provided, enforces structured output via Ollama's format parameter.\n    \"\"\"\n    request_body: dict = {\n        \"model\": model,\n        \"prompt\": prompt,\n        \"stream\": True,\n        \"options\": {\n            \"num_predict\": MAX_TOKENS,\n            \"temperature\": 0.7,\n        },\n    }\n    if json_schema:\n        request_body[\"format\"] = json_schema\n\n    payload = json.dumps(request_body).encode()"
}

**IT WORKS!** The LLM returned valid structured JSON with meaningful optimization strategies:

```json
{
  "insert": [
    {"strategy": "Batched Gemm", "priority": 1},
    {"strategy": "Parallel Reduction", "priority": 2}
  ],
  "update": {"KSPLIT": 8, "experts": 64},
  "prune": ["Unnecessary Layers"]
}

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**What just happened**: `qwen2.5-coder:7b` with Ollama's structured output produced **valid, schema-conforming JSON** in a single call — no parsing failures, no thinking blocks to strip, no markdown fences. The model proposed "Batched Gemm" and "Parallel Reduction" strategies, which are plausible (though the priorities and update format need refinement for our tree). The structured output schema enforcement eliminates the entire class of "LLM returned garbage" failures.

**Speed**: 0.2 tok/s is ~10 minutes per world model call. For overnight runs with 8-minute cycles, this means the LLM call dominates. But it's usable — unlike the 30B model that couldn't complete at all.

Returns response text or None on failure.\n\n    Uses streaming mode to avoid idle-socket timeouts on slow CPU inference.\n    Each streamed chunk resets the socket timer.\n    \"\"\"\n    payload = json.dumps({\n        \"model\": model,\n        \"prompt\": prompt,\n        \"stream\": True,\n        \"options\": {\n            \"num_predict\": MAX_TOKENS,\n            \"temperature\": 0.7,\n        },\n    }).encode()",
  "new_string": "def _call_ollama(\n    prompt: str,\n    model: str = OLLAMA_MODEL,\n    json_schema: dict | None = None,\n) -> str | None:\n    \"\"\"Call local Ollama API with streaming. Returns response text or None on failure.\n\n    Uses streaming mode to avoid idle-socket timeouts on slow CPU inference.\n    Each streamed chunk resets the socket timer.\n\n    Args:\n        json_schema: If provided, enforces structured output via Ollama's format parameter.\n    \"\"\"\n    request_body: dict = {\n        \"model\": model,\n        \"prompt\": prompt,\n        \"stream\": True,\n        \"options\": {\n            \"num_predict\": MAX_TOKENS,\n            \"temperature\": 0.7,\n        },\n    }\n    if json_schema:\n        request_body[\"format\"] = json_schema\n\n    payload = json.dumps(request_body).encode()"
}

Progress! Key observations:

1. **Code synthesis got partial output**: `LLM response in 600.7s (100 chars)` — the model generated 100 characters before the 600s timeout. Not enough for a full kernel, but it's generating.

2. **World model evolution got partial JSON**: `Expecting value: line 3 column 68 (char 83)` — 83 characters of structured JSON before timeout. The schema enforcement is working (it's producing JSON, not thinking blocks), just too slow to complete.

3. **Both timeouts = 600s (10 minutes each)** — the model is processing at ~0.15 tok/s. For the world model's ~100 token JSON output, we need ~12 minutes.

The fix is simple — the world model prompt is the one that matters most, and it needs just ~100 tokens of output. Let me bump the timeout to 900s (15min) and run one more time:

> AGENT

**THE LLM WORLD MODEL EVOLVED THE TREE!** 

```
LLM INSERT: 'E=257,d=256,bs=16: sweep KSPLIT 0-10' under root_moe
LLM UPDATE: moe_n1a priority 0.96 → 0.80
World model evolution: +1 inserted, ~1 updated, -0 pruned
```

Here's what the LLM did:

1. **INSERT**: Created a new node `"E=257,d=256,bs=16: sweep KSPLIT 0-10"` — it noticed the existing strategy only swept KSPLIT 1-6 and proposed extending the range to 0-10. That's a genuinely intelligent optimization suggestion.

2. **UPDATE**: Lowered the priority of the current node from 0.96 → 0.80 — reflecting that after 2 attempts without significant improvement, it should be deprioritized.

3. **Tree grew**: 10 → 11 active nodes, 12 total (started at 10).

The full K-Search pipeline is **working end-to-end on CPU with structured output**. 15 minutes for the world model call, which fits within the overnight cycle budget.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**What we just witnessed**: The LLM read the tree state (10 nodes, best=215.63µs), the trajectory (2 attempts on "sweep KSPLIT 1-6"), the research strategy (dead ends, priorities), and autonomously decided to:
- Propose an **extended parameter sweep** (KSPLIT 0-10 instead of 1-6) — a reasonable next step
- **Lower confidence** in the current approach (0.96→0.80) — because 2 attempts didn't break through

This is exactly the K-Search paper's core loop: **the world model learns from results and reshapes the search space**. The tree is no longer static — it evolves based on evidence.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

> AGENT

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**Model selection for parallel inference**: We need two models that (1) fit in memory together, (2) can produce structured JSON, and (3) are good enough for optimization reasoning. Best picks:
- **qwen2.5-coder:7b** (4.7GB) — proven, generated valid evolution JSON
- **deepcoder:14b** (9.0GB) — larger, better reasoning, and different architecture so no contention

Total: 13.7GB out of 128GB — plenty of headroom. But CPU bandwidth is shared, so each model will run at ~half speed. Still faster than sequential.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**GEMM got partial JSON again** — `Unterminated string at line 4 column 19 (char 40)` means it generated 40 characters of valid structured JSON in 20 minutes. With two models loaded (7B + 14B = 14GB), the bandwidth was split. The MoE run earlier with one model produced 249 chars in 15 min — exactly the bandwidth-splitting effect.

**The parallel approach works** — both models loaded and ran concurrently. But on CPU with shared bandwidth, 2 models at half-speed each ≈ same total throughput as 1 model sequentially. The real win would be with GPU acceleration or a faster CPU.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

The models ran in parallel but both timed out because the shared memory bandwidth was split between them. On this CPU hardware, sequential with one fast model is actually better than parallel with two.

The MoE demo succeeded earlier because it had the full bandwidth to itself. For GEMM and MLA, the options are:

1. **Run sequentially** with `qwen2.5-coder:7b` (proven: completes in ~15 min solo)
2. **Use an even smaller model** — `gemma3:4b` (3.3GB) might be fast enough to complete even when sharing
3. **Wait for overnight** — in the real autoresearch loop, each cycle is 8+ min anyway

Want me to run them sequentially (one after the other, same model)?

# Unsloth Kernel Optimizations

Based on the provided documentation, here's what's detailed about Unsloth's technical optimizations:

## Mentioned Optimizations

The page references that **"Unsloth's kernels optimize LoRA, FP8, FFT, PT across 500+ text, vision, TTS/audio and embedding models."** However, the document provides limited architectural specifics.

## Hardware Support

**Supported platforms:**
- NVIDIA GPUs (RTX 30, 40, 50, Blackwell, DGX systems)
- Intel GPUs (recently enabled for training)
- CPU-only inference
- macOS (chat and data processing; MLX training "coming very soon")
- AMD (chat inference works; full training support "coming soon")

## Notable Limitations

The document does **not** provide detailed information about:
- MoE (Mixture of Experts) optimization techniques
- Specific attention kernel implementations
- GEMM optimization strategies
- Quantization methodologies beyond "FP8" mention
- Triton/CUDA kernel code examples
- AMD-specific kernel development

## Open Source Components

The system leverages **"llama.cpp + Hugging Face"** infrastructure and credits several projects: PyTorch, llama.cpp, and contributions from open model communities. The core package uses Apache 2.0 licensing, while Studio UI uses AGPL-3.0.

For deeper technical details on kernel implementations, you'd need to consult the GitHub repository or additional technical documentation.

Here's a summary of the search results for **Unsloth AI's open-source Triton kernels** related to cross-entropy, LoRA, GEMM optimization on GitHub in 2026:

## Unsloth AI — Open-Source Triton Kernels & Optimizations

### Core Triton Kernel Optimizations

Unsloth replaces PyTorch operations with hand-optimized Triton kernels for critical computations including cross-entropy loss, RoPE embeddings, layer normalization, and LoRA operations. These kernels are backend-agnostic, supporting CUDA, HIP/ROCm, and Intel XPU devices. Unlike some optimization libraries, Unsloth uses exact implementations with no approximations or precision loss — all speedups come from kernel optimization, memory layout improvements, and efficient computation scheduling.

### 2026 Releases & MoE GEMM Kernels

Their first release of 2026 introduced faster MoE training, embedding model support, and ultra-long context for Reinforcement Learning. You can now train MoE models 12× faster with >35% less VRAM and 6x longer context via new Triton and math kernels (no accuracy loss). Unsloth supports fast training for gpt-oss, Qwen3 (30B, 235B, VL, Coder), DeepSeek R1/V3 arch and GLM (4.7, Flash) models.

The DeepWiki documentation for the repo confirms that the architecture includes dedicated subsystems for **Custom Triton Kernels**, **LoRA and Quantization Kernels**, and **MoE Grouped GEMM Kernels** (see [DeepWiki index](https://deepwiki.com/unslothai/unsloth)).

### Cross-Entropy Loss Kernel

At launch in Dec 2023, the team built a custom autograd engine, rewrote all kernels in OpenAI's Triton, including RoPE embeddings with optimized forward & backward passes and their own custom Fast Cross Entropy Loss kernel.

### New RoPE & MLP Triton Kernels (2026)

Unsloth now supports up to 5× faster (typically 3x) training with new custom RoPE and MLP Triton kernels, plus smart auto packing. These new kernels not only increase training speed, but also further reduce VRAM use (30%–90%) with no accuracy loss. They merged Q and K RoPE into a single Triton kernel with variable-length support, making it 2.3x faster on longer context lengths and 1.9x faster on shorter ones.

### LoRA Training Performance

Unsloth's custom kernels support optimized training for LoRA, FP8, FFT, PT and 500+ models including text, vision, audio and embeddings. They advertise training 500+ models up to 2x faster with up to 70% less VRAM, with no accuracy loss.

### Licensing

Unsloth uses a dual-licensing model of Apache 2.0 and AGPL-3.0 — the core package remains Apache 2.0, while certain optional components like Unsloth Studio UI are licensed under AGPL-3.0.

### Related Academic Work

A paper titled **"Chronicals"** on arXiv also benchmarks against Unsloth in this space. It presents fused Triton kernels that eliminate 75% of memory traffic, Cut Cross-Entropy that reduces logit memory from 5GB to 135MB, LoRA+ with differential learning rates achieving 2x faster convergence, and sequence packing.

### Key Links
- **GitHub**: [github.com/unslothai/unsloth](https://github.com/unslothai/unsloth)
- **PyPI**: [pypi.org/project/unsloth](https://pypi.org/project/unsloth/) — latest version is unsloth-2026.3.8
- **Documentation**: [docs.unsloth.ai](https://docs.unsloth.ai/new/3x-faster-training-packing)

Here are the search results for your query about Unsloth kernels, AMD ROCm, Triton, MoE, and attention optimization on GitHub:

## Key Findings

### 1. **Unsloth Main Repository** ([unslothai/unsloth](https://github.com/unslothai/unsloth))
Unsloth can train MoE LLMs 12x faster with 35% less VRAM — supporting DeepSeek, GLM, Qwen and gpt-oss. It also features new RoPE & MLP Triton Kernels & Padding Free + Packing for 3x faster training & 30% less VRAM. Regarding AMD support: AMD Chat works, and you can train with Unsloth Core, but Studio support is coming soon. Training support for Apple MLX, AMD, and Intel is listed as "coming soon."

### 2. **Unsloth MoE Release** ([Discussion #4020](https://github.com/unslothai/unsloth/discussions/4020))
You can now train MoE models 12× faster with 35% less VRAM and 6x longer context via new Triton and math kernels (no accuracy loss). gpt-oss-20b works on 12.8GB VRAM, and Qwen3-30B-A3B (16-bit LoRA) uses 63GB. Notably, there's a specific ROCm fix: "Guard torch.compile on ROCm when triton_key is missing" was contributed in PR #3923.

### 3. **TesslateAI/unsloth-rocm** ([GitHub](https://github.com/TesslateAI/unsloth-rocm))
This is a **ROCm-specific fork** of Unsloth. It supports all transformer-style models including TTS, STT, multimodal, diffusion, BERT and more. All kernels are written in OpenAI's Triton language, with a manual backprop engine and 0% loss in accuracy.

### 4. **Fused Qwen3 MoE Layer** ([Discussion #2890](https://github.com/unslothai/unsloth/discussions/2890))
A community contributor provided an example of fine-tuning fused Qwen3-30B-A3B with LoRA and 4-bit quantization, achieving 100% GPU usage and 5x speedup on a single 24GB GPU. Unsloth optimizations such as fast attention and fast LoRA, RMSNorm, and gradient checkpointing can be automatically applied.

### 5. **ROCm + Triton FlashAttention for AMD** ([Gist](https://gist.github.com/lennarkivistik/baeb6108cb189ce3b29f2269ba221eaf))
With ROCm 6.4 (March 2025), AMD introduced an AMD-Triton implementation of FlashAttention-2 for RDNA3 GPUs like the 7900 series.

### 6. **ROCm NaN Issue** ([Issue #3385](https://github.com/unslothai/unsloth/issues/3385))
Fine-tuning Gemma-3 with Unsloth on AMD Strix Halo (gfx1151) shows NaN loss from the first step, with NaNs originating in the forward pass. This indicates active work is being done on ROCm compatibility but some issues remain.

### 7. **Triton Kernel Index** ([gpu-mode/triton-index](https://github.com/gpu-mode/triton-index))
Unsloth contains many ready-to-use Triton kernels especially for finetuning applications. Applied AI contains kernels such as a Triton MoE, fused softmax for training and inference.

### 8. **Axolotl Bounty for Triton Kernels** ([Issue #1038](https://github.com/axolotl-ai-cloud/axolotl/issues/1038))
Axolotl noted that Unsloth's optimized Triton kernels significantly improve speed and memory efficiency, but only the LoRA triton kernels were open-source at the time. For Mixtral, additional kernels were requested for sparse and grouped permute_and_compute as well as gating experts.

# MoE Kernel Optimizations: Technical Details

## Core Optimization Techniques

### Grouped GEMM Implementation
The primary advancement involves PyTorch's `torch._grouped_mm` function, which replaces inefficient per-expert for-loops. The document notes that previously, "weights were stored as a `ModuleList` of per‑expert linear layers," requiring sequential expert processing. The new approach consolidates expert weights into a single `nn.Parameter`, enabling parallel computation across experts.

**Hardware Support:** Works on GPUs from NVIDIA T4 through B200, with verification on H100, A100, and RTX 6000 Pro systems.

### Triton Kernel Optimizations
Unsloth developed custom Triton kernels achieving **~2.5× speedup versus `torch._grouped_mm` on A100 GPUs**. Key characteristics include:

- One-time autotune phase (~2 minutes) selecting optimal kernel configurations
- **35% faster overall training** on A100 versus baseline implementations
- Backward compatibility with older PyTorch versions and A100 hardware

### Split LoRA Architecture
The document describes a memory-efficient approach avoiding full LoRA weight materialization. Instead of computing `delta = loraA @ loraB` (materializing intermediate results), the system performs sequential matrix operations:

```
Y = X @ loraA  # sparse computation across k routed experts
Z = Y @ loraB  # subsequent projection
```

This reordering uses **matrix-multiplication associativity** while maintaining identical loss, gradients, and outputs.

**Memory advantage:** For Qwen3-30B-A3B (E=128 experts, k=8 activated, m=2048, n=768), Split LoRA advantages emerge when `s < 32K tokens`, where `s` represents sequence length.

## Performance Characteristics

### Benchmark Results Summary

**gpt-oss-20B (BF16):**
- 7× faster training at 16K context
- 36% VRAM reduction
- Transformers v5 OOMs while Unsloth handles increased sequence lengths

**Qwen3-30B-A3B (B200):**
- ~1.7× speedup with ~35% memory efficiency gains
- Improved memory scaling at longer sequences

**GLM 4.7 Flash (A100):**
- 2.6× faster throughput
- >15% VRAM reduction across batch sizes

### Computational Complexity Analysis

The document provides formal FLOP comparisons. PEFT's standard approach requires `E(2mnr + mn) + 2k·smn` operations per batch, while Split LoRA performs `2k(smn + smr + srn)` operations, with crossover around `s ≈ 16K tokens` for comparable models.

## Backend Selection

Unsloth implements automatic backend selection via environment variables:

```
UNSLOTH_MOE_BACKEND = "grouped_mm"     # Default; T4+ support
UNSLOTH_MOE_BACKEND = "unsloth_triton" # A100 optimized
UNSLOTH_MOE_BACKEND = "native_torch"   # Fallback (12× slower)
```

## Notable Limitations

**AMD/ROCm Compatibility:** The document contains **no mention of AMD GPU or ROCm support**. All benchmarks exclusively reference NVIDIA hardware (T4, H100, A100, B200, RTX 6000 Pro).

**QLoRA Constraints:** "Training MoE models in 4-bit QLoRA isn't recommended right now because BitsandBytes doesn't support it." Users must employ bf16 for LoRA or full fine-tuning instead.

## Code Integration Example

```python
os.environ['UNSLOTH_MOE_BACKEND'] = 'grouped_mm'
model = FastLanguageModel.from_pretrained(
    model_name="Qwen/Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507",
    load_in_4bit=False  # Required for MoE nn.Parameter
)
model = FastLanguageModel.get_peft_model(
    model,
    r=lora_rank,
    target_modules=["q_proj", "k_proj", "v_proj", "o_proj", 
                    "gate_up_proj", "down_proj"]
)
```

## Absence of Implementation Details

The document deliberately omits low-level kernel code, token permutation algorithms, memory layout specifications, and split-K strategies. It provides conceptual frameworks rather than reproducible implementation details.

# SPDX-License-Identifier: GNU Affero General Public License v3.0
# Copyright 2023-present the Unsloth team. All rights reserved.

import torch
import triton
import triton.language as tl

from .autotuning import (
    get_forward_configs,
    prune_kernel_configs_fwd,
)


#
# PERMUTE_X -> permute tokens so that they are ordered by expert
# PERMUTE_Y -> permute output so that they are ordered by token
# These are effectively the same thing: the former loads in permuted order, the latter stores in permuted order => we only need to define the permutation indices once
# In the former, we use these row indices when loading X
# For the latter, we use these row indices when storing Y
# FUSE_MUL -> multiply routed outputs by their respective weights
# topk_weights are in token order
# Only account for the case when X is in expert order and we are permuting Y when fusing mul -- this precondition is checked in the interface
@triton.jit
def _grouped_gemm_forward_kernel(
    x_ptr,
    w_ptr,
    y_ptr,
    # Variable depending on routed probs
    m_sizes_ptr,
    gather_indices_ptr,
    topk_weights_ptr,
    # Constant problem shapes
    NUM_EXPERTS: tl.constexpr,
    NUM_TOKENS,
    TOPK: tl.constexpr,
    N: tl.constexpr,
    K: tl.constexpr,
    NUM_SMS,
    # Tuning params
    BLOCK_SIZE_M: tl.constexpr,
    BLOCK_SIZE_N: tl.constexpr,
    BLOCK_SIZE_K: tl.constexpr,
    PERMUTE_X: tl.constexpr = False,
    PERMUTE_Y: tl.constexpr = False,
    FUSE_MUL_PRE: tl.constexpr = False,
    FUSE_MUL_POST: tl.constexpr = False,
    USE_FAST_ACCUM: tl.constexpr = False,
    USE_TMA_LOAD_W: tl.constexpr = False,
    USE_TMA_LOAD_X: tl.constexpr = False,
    USE_TMA_STORE: tl.constexpr = False,
    acc_dtype: tl.constexpr = tl.float32,
    FLATTEN: tl.constexpr = True,
) -> None:
    tl.static_assert(K % BLOCK_SIZE_K == 0)

    TOTAL_TOKENS = NUM_TOKENS * TOPK
    SHOULD_PERMUTE: tl.constexpr = PERMUTE_X or PERMUTE_Y
    SHOULD_FUSE_MUL: tl.constexpr = FUSE_MUL_PRE or FUSE_MUL_POST
    SHOULD_PERMUTE_OR_FUSE: tl.constexpr = SHOULD_PERMUTE or SHOULD_FUSE_MUL
    # tl.static_print("SHOULD_PERMUTE", PERMUTE_X, PERMUTE_Y, FUSE_MUL_PRE, FUSE_MUL_POST, SHOULD_PERMUTE, SHOULD_FUSE, SHOULD_PERMUTE_OR_FUSE)
    tidx = tl.program_id(0)
    output_dtype: tl.dtype = y_ptr.dtype.element_ty

    # Create TMA descriptors for loading sorted tokens
    # When using TMA load, we don't permute_x, so shape should be [TOTAL_TOKENS, K]
    # Also, we are defining a single global descriptor with single block shape
    # Need to check that this does not result in errors when crossing expert boundaries
    if USE_TMA_LOAD_X:
        x_desc = tl.make_tensor_descriptor(
            x_ptr,
            shape = [TOTAL_TOKENS, K],
            strides = [K, 1],
            block_shape = [BLOCK_SIZE_M, BLOCK_SIZE_K],
        )

    if USE_TMA_LOAD_W:
        expert_stride = N * K
        w_desc = tl.make_tensor_descriptor(
            w_ptr,
            shape = [NUM_EXPERTS, N, K],
            strides = [expert_stride, K, 1],
            block_shape = [1, BLOCK_SIZE_N, BLOCK_SIZE_K],
        )

    m_end = 0
    processed_tiles = 0
    m_block_range = tl.arange(0, BLOCK_SIZE_M)

    for expert_idx in tl.range(NUM_EXPERTS, flatten = FLATTEN):
        m_start = m_end
        m_size = tl.load(m_sizes_ptr + expert_idx).to(tl.int32)
        m_end = m_start + m_size

        if m_size > 0:
            n_start = expert_idx * N

            num_m_tiles = tl.cdiv(m_size, BLOCK_SIZE_M)
            num_n_tiles = tl.cdiv(N, BLOCK_SIZE_N)
            num_tiles_per_expert = num_m_tiles * num_n_tiles

            # Need to create tma_store within loop since we need to predicate stores based on m_size
            if USE_TMA_STORE:
                y_desc = tl.make_tensor_descriptor(
                    y_ptr,  # + m_start * N,
                    shape = [m_end, N],
                    strides = [N, 1],
                    block_shape = [BLOCK_SIZE_M, BLOCK_SIZE_N],
                )

            # Process tiles for this expert
            while (
                tidx >= processed_tiles
                and tidx < processed_tiles + num_tiles_per_expert
            ):
                tile_idx = tidx - processed_tiles

                # Check if L2 cache re-use for this order is optimal
                tile_m_idx = tile_idx % num_m_tiles
                tile_n_idx = tile_idx // num_m_tiles

                if SHOULD_PERMUTE_OR_FUSE:
                    # These will be used for loading and storing in permuted order
                    gather_offsets = tile_m_idx * BLOCK_SIZE_M + m_block_range
                    indices_to_gather = m_start + tl.max_contiguous(
                        tl.multiple_of(gather_offsets % m_size, BLOCK_SIZE_M),
                        BLOCK_SIZE_M,
                    )
                    expert_token_idx = tl.load(
                        gather_indices_ptr + indices_to_gather,
                        mask = indices_to_gather < TOTAL_TOKENS,
                    )
                    expert_token_offsets = expert_token_idx[:, None]

                    # Masks for permuted load and store

                    row_mask = gather_offsets < m_size
                    row_mask = row_mask[:, None]

                    # row_mask = indices_to_gather < m_end
                    # row_mask = row_mask[:, None]

                # We only take into account the following two cases: (PERMUTE_X and NOT PERMUTE_Y) and (NOT PERMUTE_X and PERMUTE_Y)
                # Hence, we can make the following simplifying assumptions when loading and storing
                # Note the different strides between the two cases: the offsets for loading and storing are flipped and the strides must also be adjusted
                if PERMUTE_X:
                    load_idx = (
                        (expert_token_offsets // TOPK) * K
                    )  # Permute on load from token -> expert order, divide by TOPK to index from original number of tokens
                    store_idx = (
                        indices_to_gather[:, None] * N

# SPDX-License-Identifier: GNU Affero General Public License v3.0
# Copyright 2023-present the Unsloth team. All rights reserved.

import logging
import warnings
from dataclasses import asdict
from unsloth import DEVICE_TYPE

import torch
import triton

from .kernels.backward import (
    _autotuned_grouped_gemm_dW_kernel,
    _autotuned_grouped_gemm_dX_kernel,
    _grouped_gemm_dW_kernel,
    _grouped_gemm_dX_kernel,
)
from .kernels.forward import (
    _autotuned_grouped_gemm_forward_kernel,
    _grouped_gemm_forward_kernel,
)
from .kernels.tuning import (
    KernelConfigBackward_dW,
    KernelConfigBackward_dX,
    KernelConfigForward,
)

logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)
# Set formatter to include timestamp, pathname and lineno
formatter = logging.Formatter(
    "%(asctime)s::%(levelname)s,%(pathname)s:%(lineno)d:: %(message)s"
)

# Add console handler
ch = logging.StreamHandler()
ch.setFormatter(formatter)
logger.addHandler(ch)


# Precompute TMA support to avoid graph breaks
# TMA requires both:
# 1. NVIDIA GPU with capability >= 9 (Hopper+)
# 2. Triton version with TMA API (make_tensor_descriptor or _experimental_make_tensor_descriptor)
def _check_tma_support():
    if DEVICE_TYPE in ("xpu", "hip"):
        return False
    import triton.language as tl

    gpu_supports_tma = torch.cuda.get_device_capability()[0] >= 9
    # Check for both old experimental and new stable API names
    triton_has_tma_api = hasattr(tl, "make_tensor_descriptor") or hasattr(
        tl, "_experimental_make_tensor_descriptor"
    )
    return gpu_supports_tma and triton_has_tma_api


_SUPPORTS_TMA = _check_tma_support()

# Check if triton.set_allocator is available (Triton 3.0+)
_HAS_SET_ALLOCATOR = hasattr(triton, "set_allocator")


def supports_tma():
    return _SUPPORTS_TMA


# Helper to support allow_in_graph
try:
    from torch.compiler import allow_in_graph
except ImportError:
    from torch._dynamo import allow_in_graph


# Helper to detect if we're in tracing/compilation mode
def _is_tracing(*tensors):
    """
    Check if tensors are fake tensors used during torch.compile tracing.
    During tracing, tensors are FakeTensor/FunctionalTensor and we can't run Triton kernels.
    During execution, tensors are real Tensors and we MUST run the kernels.

    NOTE: We do NOT use torch.compiler.is_compiling() because it returns True
    during both tracing AND execution. We only want to skip kernels during tracing
    when tensors are actually fake.
    """
    for t in tensors:
        name = type(t).__name__
        if name in ("FakeTensor", "FunctionalTensor", "FunctionalTensorWrapper"):
            return True
    return False


_per_device_alloc_fns = {}


def get_per_device_per_stream_alloc_fn(device):
    if device not in _per_device_alloc_fns:
        _per_stream_tensors = {}

        def alloc_fn(size: int, alignment: int, stream):
            assert alignment == 128

    if val is None:

    elif isinstance(val, list):

    import triton.language as tl

    return hasattr(tl, "make_tensor_descriptor") or hasattr(
        tl, "_experimental_make_tensor_descriptor"
    )

> AGENT

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**Unsloth's MoE kernel architecture is impressive but has key limitations for our use case**:

1. **Grouped GEMM in Triton** — Their `_grouped_gemm_forward_kernel` is a proper persistent-tile Triton kernel with per-expert token permutation, fused weight multiplication, and TMA descriptor support. The tile sizes (M=64/128, N=64/128/256, K=64/128/256) with autotuning give them 2.5x over `torch._grouped_mm`.

2. **Token permutation is fused** — They fuse the scatter/gather (token-to-expert sorting) directly into the GEMM kernel via `PERMUTE_X`/`PERMUTE_Y` flags. This eliminates the separate permutation pass that `aiter.fused_moe` does.

3. **Split LoRA** — Their matrix associativity trick (`Y = X @ loraA; Z = Y @ loraB` instead of `delta = loraA @ loraB; Y = X @ delta`) saves memory for fine-tuning but isn't relevant for inference.

4. **AGPL-3.0 license** — The kernel code is AGPL, not Apache. We can study their techniques but can't copy code into our competition submission.

5. **No AMD/ROCm support for MoE kernels** — `_check_tma_support()` explicitly returns `False` for `"hip"`. Their grouped GEMM kernels are NVIDIA-only (TMA requires Hopper+). However, the **non-TMA path** (standard Triton loads) would work on AMD.

6. **Key technique we can learn**: The persistent-tile scheduling (`while tidx >= processed_tiles`) avoids re-launching kernels per expert. Each SM walks through experts in a single kernel launch. This is exactly what `aiter.fused_moe` does internally via CK ASM, but Unsloth does it in portable Triton.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

## Can We Use Unsloth's Kernel Optimizations?

### Directly usable techniques (learn, don't copy — AGPL):

| Technique | Unsloth Implementation | Our Application |
|-----------|----------------------|-----------------|
| **Fused token permutation** | `PERMUTE_X`/`PERMUTE_Y` in GEMM kernel | Could fuse sorting into our MoE kernel, eliminating separate `moe_sorting_fwd` call |
| **Persistent-tile scheduling** | `while tidx >= processed_tiles` loop | Avoids per-expert kernel launches; reduces dispatch overhead |
| **Autotuning tile sizes** | M=64/128, N=64/128/256, K=64/128/256 | Good starting configs for AMD MI355X Triton kernels |
| **Expert-aware memory layout** | Contiguous expert blocks with `m_sizes_ptr` | Better L2 cache utilization for sparse expert distributions |

### Not applicable:

| Technique | Why Not |
|-----------|---------|
| TMA descriptors | Hopper-only (NVIDIA SM90+), not on AMD |
| Split LoRA | Training optimization, we're doing inference |
| `torch._grouped_mm` | Our runner uses `aiter.fused_moe` which is already CK-optimized |

### The big opportunity:

**Write a custom Triton MoE kernel for AMD MI355X** using Unsloth's architectural patterns (persistent tiles, fused permutation) but adapted for AMD's XCD topology (8 compute dies) and `tl.dot_scaled` for MXFP4. This would bypass `aiter.fused_moe`'s Python dispatch overhead entirely.

This is a node we should add to the K-Search tree. Want me to add "Custom Triton MoE with fused permutation (Unsloth-inspired)" as a new LLM-proposed strategy?

- [Unsloth ROCm Fork](https://github.com/TesslateAI/unsloth-rocm)
- [DeepWiki Architecture](https://deepwiki.com/unslothai/unsloth)

MoE: direct `ck_moe_stage1`/`ck_moe_stage2` calling conventions\n4. GEMM: Triton `tl.dot_scaled` with correct scale layout [BLOCK_N, SCALE_PER_K]\n5. MLA: FlashAttention-style fused tiling (eliminates 3-stage pipeline overhead)\n\n## Reference: Unsloth Grouped GEMM MoE Kernel (AGPL-3.0 — study only, do NOT copy code)\n\nSource: github.com/unslothai/unsloth/tree/main/unsloth/kernels/moe/grouped_gemm/\n\n### Architecture (applicable to our MoE kernel)\n\n**Persistent-tile scheduling**: Single kernel launch walks all experts. Each SM iterates\n`for expert_idx in range(NUM_EXPERTS)`, computing tiles within each expert. Avoids per-expert\nkernel launch overhead that aiter.fused_moe has via Python dispatch.\n\n```\n# Pseudocode (NOT Unsloth code — our own interpretation of the pattern)\nfor expert in range(NUM_EXPERTS):\n    m_size = token_counts[expert]        # tokens routed to this expert\n    num_tiles = cdiv(m_size, BLOCK_M) * cdiv(N, BLOCK_N)\n    while my_tile_id in range(processed, processed + num_tiles):\n        # compute GEMM tile for this expert\n        processed += num_tiles\n```\n\n**Fused token permutation**: Token sorting (scatter/gather) is fused INTO the GEMM kernel\nvia `PERMUTE_X` (permute on load) or `PERMUTE_Y` (permute on store). Eliminates the separate\n`moe_sorting_fwd` call. Gather indices are loaded per-tile:\n- `gather_indices_ptr` maps sorted position → original token position\n- Load X in expert-sorted order, store Y in token order (or vice versa)\n\n**Autotuning config space**: BLOCK_M=[64,128], BLOCK_N=[64,128,256], BLOCK_K=[64,128,256],\nnum_warps=[4,8], num_stages=[3,4,5]. ~2 min autotune phase, results cached.\n\n### What We Can Apply on AMD MI355X\n\n| Unsloth Technique | AMD Adaptation | Expected Impact |\n|-------------------|----------------|-----------------|\n| Persistent tiles | Same pattern in Triton (works on HIP) | Eliminates per-expert dispatch (~5-10µs) |\n| Fused permutation | Replace `moe_sorting_fwd` + `fused_moe` with single kernel | Saves one kernel launch + memory round-trip |\n| Autotuned tile sizes | Adapt for gfx950 XCD topology (8 compute dies) | May need XCD-aware remapping (see dead ends) |\n| `tl.dot_scaled` for MXFP4 | Use instead of `tl.dot` for native FP4 support | Hardware MXFP4 acceleration on MI355X |\n\n### What Does NOT Transfer\n\n- **TMA descriptors**: Hopper-only (SM90+), not on AMD. Use standard `tl.load`/`tl.store`.\n- **Split LoRA**: Training optimization, irrelevant for inference kernels.\n- **`torch._grouped_mm`**: CUDA-only backend, not available on ROCm.\n\n### Implementation Sketch (for K-Search world model)\n\nA custom Triton MoE kernel for MI355X would:\n1. Accept pre-sorted tokens + expert counts (from `moe_sorting_fwd` or custom sort)\n2. Use persistent-tile loop over experts (avoid per-expert kernel launch)\n3. Use `tl.dot_scaled` for native MXFP4 GEMM (see `amd-gfx950-tl-dot-scaled-constraints`)\n4. Fuse the gate weight multiplication into the store phase (`FUSE_MUL_POST`)\n5. Handle XCD remapping carefully (see `tritonblas-origami-xcd-remapping-bug` skill)\n\nRisk: Custom Triton MoE was previously 68% slower than CK ASM (dead end). The difference\nnow is (a) fused permutation was not tried, (b) `tl.dot_scaled` for MXFP4 was not used,\nand (c) persistent-tile scheduling was not applied. These three combined may close the gap."

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**Why this matters for the competition**: Our MoE gap is only 1.07x (155µs vs 145µs leader). The Unsloth-inspired approach combines three techniques that weren't used in the previous failed custom Triton attempt: (1) persistent tiles eliminate per-expert dispatch overhead (~5-10µs), (2) fused permutation removes a full memory round-trip, and (3) `tl.dot_scaled` gives native MXFP4 hardware acceleration. Even if each saves only 3-5µs, the combined effect could close the 10µs gap to the leader. The K-Search world model will now prioritize this path and propose implementation variants automatically.

> AGENT

All 4 cycles ran but the LLM timed out on every one — partial JSON each time (33-68 chars). This run started before the earlier sequential runs that loaded the model, so the model was cold and competing with those other runs for bandwidth.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**The 20-minute timeout is right at the edge for this hardware.** The successful runs earlier (MoE: 15 min, GEMM: 15 min, MLA: 17 min) worked because the model was warm and solo. This 4-cycle run had the model competing with the parallel GEMM/MLA runs we launched, plus each cycle needed to re-warm after the previous one's prompt eval.

**The deterministic loop still works perfectly** — 4 cycles, 3 kernels, 11 total attempts, trees updated with priorities, convergence tracking active. The LLM just couldn't finish in time. For overnight runs, we'd set `OLLAMA_TIMEOUT=1800` (30 min) which would be plenty.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Here's the run summary:

| Cycle | Kernel | Selected Node | Synthetic Result | LLM Evolution |
|-------|--------|---------------|-----------------|---------------|
| 1 | MoE | KSPLIT 0-6 (bs=128) | 223.3µs | Timeout (1 char) |
| 2 | MLA | splits 2-16 (bs=4) | 161.5µs | Timeout (68 chars) |
| 3 | MoE | KSPLIT 0-6 (bs=128) | 230.4µs | Timeout (39 chars) |
| 4 | GEMM | A-quant caching | 177.9µs | Timeout (33 chars) |

**Final tree state**: MoE 11 active/5 attempts, GEMM 13 active/3 attempts, MLA 12 active/3 attempts.

The trees are accumulating synthetic data correctly. The weighted kernel selection (50% MoE, 30% GEMM, 20% MLA) gave us MoE 2x, MLA 1x, GEMM 1x — matching the weights. For a real overnight run with `OLLAMA_TIMEOUT=1800` and solo model access, the LLM would complete every evolution call.

     1→# Custom Triton MoE Kernel Specification
     2→
     3→**Created:** 2026-03-20
     4→**Status:** READY FOR IMPLEMENTATION
     5→**Task:** Replace aiter.fused_moe with a custom Triton kernel using persistent tiles + fused permutation
     6→
     7→---
     8→
     9→## 1. Problem Statement
    10→
    11→aiter's `fused_moe` sits at ~155µs (geomean over 7 benchmark shapes); leader is 145µs (1.07x gap).
    12→All Python-level knobs on `fused_moe` are exhausted. The hypothesis for closing the gap:
    13→
    14→1. **Persistent tile scheduling** — avoid per-expert kernel launch overhead
    15→2. **Fused token permutation** — eliminate separate `moe_sorting_fwd` call + memory round-trip
    16→3. **Native MXFP4 via `tl.dot_scaled`** — hardware-accelerated FP4 GEMM on MI355X gfx950
    17→
    18→The approach is inspired by Unsloth's grouped GEMM MoE kernel (AGPL-3.0 — study only, no copying).
    19→
    20→---
    21→
    22→## 2. Exact Competition Interface
    23→
    24→### Input tuple (12 elements)
    25→
    26→```python
    27→input_t = tuple of:
    28→  [0]  hidden_states              [M, d_hidden]                         bf16
    29→  [1]  gate_up_weight             [E, 2*d_expert_pad, d_hidden_pad//2]  fp4x2  (raw, UN-shuffled)
    30→  [2]  down_weight                [E, d_hidden_pad, d_expert_pad//2]    fp4x2  (raw, UN-shuffled)
    31→  [3]  gate_up_weight_scale       [E, 2*d_expert_pad, scale_K]          e8m0   (raw, UN-shuffled)
    32→  [4]  down_weight_scale          [E, d_hidden_pad, scale_K]            e8m0   (raw, UN-shuffled)
    33→  [5]  gate_up_weight_shuffled    [E, 2*d_expert_pad, d_hidden_pad//2]  fp4x2  (pre-shuffled for CK)
    34→  [6]  down_weight_shuffled       [E, d_hidden_pad, d_expert_pad//2]    fp4x2  (pre-shuffled for CK)
    35→  [7]  gate_up_weight_scale_shuffled  [padded, flat]                    e8m0   (pre-shuffled for CK)
    36→  [8]  down_weight_scale_shuffled     [padded, flat]                    e8m0   (pre-shuffled for CK)
    37→  [9]  topk_weights               [M, total_top_k]                      float32
    38→  [10] topk_ids                   [M, total_top_k]                      int32
    39→  [11] config                     dict with keys:
    40→         d_hidden, d_expert, d_hidden_pad, d_expert_pad,
    41→         n_routed_experts, n_shared_experts, n_experts_per_token,
    42→         total_top_k, bs
    43→```
    44→
    45→### Output
    46→
    47→```python
    48→output: [M, d_hidden] bf16
    49→```
    50→
    51→### Key computed values
    52→
    53→```python
    54→M          = hidden_states.shape[0]        # number of tokens
    55→E          = gate_up_weight.shape[0]       # total experts (routed + shared)
    56→top_k      = topk_ids.shape[1]             # tokens per expert assignment
    57→d_hidden   = config["d_hidden"]            # e.g. 4096 or 7168
    58→d_expert   = config["d_expert"]            # e.g. 256, 512, 1536, 2048
    59→d_hidden_pad  = config["d_hidden_pad"]     # padded to 256-align
    60→d_expert_pad  = config["d_expert_pad"]     # padded to 256-align
    61→hidden_pad    = d_hidden_pad - d_hidden
    62→intermediate_pad = d_expert_pad - d_expert
    63→```
    64→
    65→### Benchmark shapes (March 2026, subject to change)
    66→
    67→| E (total) | n_routed | d_expert | bs  | top_k | d_hidden |
    68→|-----------|----------|----------|-----|-------|----------|
    69→| 257       | 256      | 256      | 16  | 9     | 4096     |
    70→| 257       | 256      | 256      | 128 | 9     | 4096     |
    71→| 257       | 256      | 256      | 512 | 9     | 4096     |
    72→| 33        | 32       | 512      | 16  | 9     | 7168     |
    73→| 33        | 32       | 512      | 128 | 9     | 7168     |
    74→| 33        | 32       | 512      | 512 | 9     | 7168     |
    75→| 33        | 32       | 2048     | 512 | 9     | 7168     |
    76→
    77→---
    78→
    79→## 3. aiter fused_moe Dispatch Flow (What We Are Replacing)
    80→
    81→Understanding the aiter pipeline enables targeted replacement.
    82→
    83→### 3.1 fused_moe Python wrapper
    84→
    85→```
    86→fused_moe(hidden_states, w1_shuffled, w2_shuffled, topk_weights, topk_ids, ...)
    87→  ├── Compute: estimated_m = topk_ids.numel() // num_experts
    88→  ├── Lookup: CSV config (dsv3_fp4_tuned_fmoe.csv) — competition shapes have ZERO matches
    89→  ├── Heuristic path (always taken for competition shapes):
    90→  │     ksplit = env["AITER_KSPLIT"] if set (default 0)
    91→  │     block_m = auto-selected (32 for small bs, 64 for larger)
    92→  ├── Allocate: sorted_token_ids, sorted_weights, sorted_expert_ids, num_valid_ids, moe_buf
    93→  ├── Call: moe_sorting_fwd(topk_ids, topk_weights, → sorted outputs, num_experts, block_m)
    94→  └── Branch:
    95→        KSPLIT=0 (CK path):
    96→          Call: ck_moe_stage1(hidden_states, w1, w2, sorted_token_ids,
    97→                              sorted_expert_ids, num_valid_ids, out, topk,
    98→                              w1_scale, is_shuffled=True, quant_type=0, activation=0, splitk=1)
    99→          → output [M, top_k, N] bf16 (SiLU already fused)
   100→          Call: ck_moe_stage2(out, w2, sorted_token_ids, topk_weights, ...)
   101→          → output [M, d_hidden] bf16
   102→
   103→        KSPLIT>0 (cktile path):
   104→          Call: moe_cktile2stages_gemm1(hidden_q, w1, out, sorted_ids, ...)
   105→          If split_k>0: Call silu_and_mul(out, tmp)
   106→          Call: fused_dynamic_mxfp4_quant_moe_sort(intermediate, sorted_ids, ...)
   107→          Call: moe_cktile2stages_gemm2(intermediate_q, w2, out, sorted_ids, ...)
   108→```
   109→
   110→### 3.2 moe_sorting_fwd outputs
   111→
   112→```python
   113→moe_sorting_fwd(
   114→    topk_ids,           # [M, top_k] int32  — expert IDs per token
   115→    topk_weights,       # [M, top_k] float32
   116→    sorted_token_ids,   # OUTPUT: [E * block_m] int32 — token positions in expert-sorted order
   117→    sorted_weights,     # OUTPUT: [E * block_m] float32 — weights in expert-sorted order
   118→    sorted_expert_ids,  # OUTPUT: [ceil(E*block_m / block_m)] int32 — expert ID per tile
   119→    num_valid_ids,      # OUTPUT: [1] int32 — total non-padding token slots
   120→    moe_buf,            # workspace [M * top_k + E * block_m + 1] int32 (estimated)
   121→    num_experts,        # int
   122→    unit_size,          # int (= block_m, tile size in M dimension)
   123→    local_expert_mask=None,  # Optional[Tensor] — EP-mode masking (DO NOT USE)
   124→    num_local_tokens=None,
   125→    dispatch_policy=0,
   126→)
   127→```
   128→
   129→**sorted_token_ids** (`[E * block_m]`): Each expert gets `block_m` slots. Position `i` contains:
   130→- A valid token index (0..M*top_k-1) if a token was routed to this slot
   131→- A padding value (>= M*top_k) if the slot is empty
   132→
   133→**sorted_expert_ids** (`[ceil(E*block_m / block_m)]`): One entry per tile (one tile = block_m rows).
   134→Entry `t` is the expert ID for that tile. Length = `ceil((E * block_m) / block_m) = E`.
   135→
   136→**num_valid_ids** (`[1]`): Total count of NON-padding entries in sorted_token_ids.
   137→The CK kernel uses this to avoid processing empty tail tiles.
   138→
   139→**Key property**: This is a compact sorted permutation. Token `j` routed to expert `e` appears
   140→in `sorted_token_ids[e * block_m + offset]` for some offset < block_m.
   141→
   142→### 3.3 What Python overhead we can eliminate
   143→
   144→The key overhead in fused_moe's Python dispatch (per call):
   145→- moe_sorting_fwd itself: ~5-10µs (separate kernel launch + small tensors)
   146→- Python wrapper logic: ~2-3µs (shape inference, alloc, env var checks)
   147→- Two separate kernel launches (stage1 + stage2): ~2µs launch overhead each
   148→
   149→A fused Triton kernel eliminates sorting as a separate pass and fuses it into the load phase.
   150→
   151→---
   152→
   153→## 4. Weight Tensor Layout for Triton (Raw Path)
   154→
   155→The custom Triton kernel uses the **raw (un-shuffled)** weights from inputs [1]-[4],
   156→NOT the pre-shuffled variants [5]-[8] (which are formatted for CK hardware tiles).
   157→
   158→### gate_up_weight (raw): `[E, 2*d_expert_pad, d_hidden_pad//2]` fp4x2
   159→
   160→- Dtype on disk: `torch.REDACTED` (2 fp4 values packed per byte)
   161→- For Triton: view as `torch.uint8` → shape `[E, 2*d_expert_pad, d_hidden_pad//2]`
   162→- Each uint8 holds 2 fp4 values: high nibble = fp4[0], low nibble = fp4[1]
   163→- Row-major layout: last dim is K (hidden), second dim is N (expert intermediate)
   164→- First half (`[:, :d_expert_pad, :]`) is gate weights; second half is up weights
   165→
   166→### down_weight (raw): `[E, d_hidden_pad, d_expert_pad//2]` fp4x2
   167→
   168→- Similar packing: view as `torch.uint8`
   169→- Shape after view: `[E, d_hidden_pad, d_expert_pad//2]`
   170→- Row-major: last dim is K (expert intermediate), second dim is N (hidden output)
   171→
   172→### gate_up_weight_scale (raw): `[E, 2*d_expert_pad, scale_K]` e8m0
   173→
   174→- `scale_K = ceil(d_hidden_pad / 32)` — one scale per 32 fp4 elements along K
   175→- Dtype: `torch.float8_e8m0fnu` → view as `torch.uint8` for Triton
   176→- Layout: `[E, N_rows, K_scales]` where N_rows=2*d_expert_pad, K_scales=d_hidden_pad//32
   177→
   178→### How to recover un-shuffled scales from shuffled inputs (CRITICAL)
   179→
   180→The competition provides pre-shuffled scales `w1ssh` / `w2ssh` but NOT raw scales directly
   181→in a Triton-compatible layout. However raw scales ARE available as `w1s` / `w2s` (inputs [3],[4]).
   182→
   183→**Triton-compatible scale layout** for `tl.dot_scaled`:
   184→- A-scale (activation): `[M_tokens, K//32]` uint8
   185→- B-scale (weight): `[N_rows, K//32]` uint8 — N-first (NOT transposed)
   186→
   187→The raw `gate_up_weight_scale [E, 2*d_expert_pad, scale_K]` is already in this layout per-expert.
   188→For expert `e`, slice `[e, :, :]` gives `[2*d_expert_pad, scale_K]` uint8 → correct B-scale layout.
   189→
   190→---
   191→
   192→## 5. Token Permutation Data Flow (Fused Approach)
   193→
   194→The key innovation over aiter: fuse sorting INTO the kernel load phase.
   195→
   196→### 5.1 Pre-computation (Python, before kernel launch)
   197→
   198→```python
   199→# Flatten topk_ids to [M * top_k] — token-expert pairs
   200→flat_ids = topk_ids.view(-1)       # [M * top_k] int32
   201→flat_weights = topk_weights.view(-1)  # [M * top_k] float32
   202→
   203→# For each token-expert pair j, flat_ids[j] is the expert, j//top_k is the original token
   204→
   205→# Sort by expert ID to group tokens per expert
   206→sort_order = torch.argsort(flat_ids)   # [M * top_k] int64 — indices into flat_ids
   207→sorted_experts = flat_ids[sort_order]  # [M * top_k] int32 — expert IDs, sorted
   208→sorted_token_pos = (sort_order // top_k).to(torch.int32)  # original token index per sorted slot
   209→sorted_weights_flat = flat_weights[sort_order]  # [M * top_k] float32
   210→
   211→# Count tokens per expert
   212→tokens_per_expert = torch.bincount(flat_ids, minlength=E).to(torch.int32)  # [E]
   213→expert_offsets = torch.zeros(E+1, dtype=torch.int32, device=device)
   214→expert_offsets[1:] = tokens_per_expert.cumsum(0)  # [E+1] start offset per expert
   215→```
   216→
   217→This Python-side sorting replaces `moe_sorting_fwd`. The kernel receives:
   218→- `sorted_token_pos [M*top_k]`: position in original `hidden_states` for each sorted slot
   219→- `sorted_weights_flat [M*top_k]`: topk weight for each sorted slot
   220→- `expert_offsets [E+1]`: start/end positions in sorted arrays per expert
   221→
   222→### 5.2 In-kernel gather (PERMUTE_X pattern)
   223→
   224→For expert `e` processing tokens `[expert_offsets[e] : expert_offsets[e+1]]`:
   225→```triton
   226→# Gather token from original hidden_states using sorted_token_pos
   227→orig_pos = tl.load(sorted_token_pos_ptr + sort_idx)  # scalar: original token index
   228→x = tl.load(hidden_states_ptr + orig_pos * stride_hs_m + k_offs * stride_hs_k)
   229→```
   230→
   231→This eliminates a full scatter/gather pass — tokens are read in expert-sorted order directly.
   232→
   233→### 5.3 Store with weight fusion (PERMUTE_Y pattern)
   234→
   235→After computing expert output `y [block_m, d_hidden]`:
   236→```triton
   237→# Scatter back to original output positions with topk_weight multiplication
   238→weight = tl.load(sorted_weights_ptr + sort_idx)  # topk weight for this slot
   239→# Atomic add back to output (or use sorted order + reduction pass)
   240→tl.atomic_add(out_ptr + orig_pos * stride_out_m + n_offs, y * weight[:, None])
   241→```
   242→
   243→**Note**: Atomic adds are required because multiple experts write to the same output token.
   244→This is the key complexity vs aiter's approach (which uses a separate reduce pass).
   245→
   246→---
   247→
   248→## 6. Persistent Tile Scheduling (Kernel Architecture)
   249→
   250→### 6.1 Stage 1: gate+up projection (gate_up_weight, d_hidden → d_expert)
   251→
   252→One kernel walk for ALL experts:
   253→
   254→```python
   255→# Grid: (num_sms,) — one program per SM, persistent scheduling
   256→# Each SM walks: expert 0 tiles, expert 1 tiles, ..., expert E-1 tiles
   257→
   258→@triton.jit
   259→def moe_stage1_kernel(
   260→    hidden_states_ptr,     # [M, d_hidden] bf16
   261→    gate_up_weight_ptr,    # [E, 2*d_expert_pad, d_hidden_pad//2] uint8 (fp4x2)
   262→    gate_up_scale_ptr,     # [E, 2*d_expert_pad, scale_K] uint8 (e8m0)
   263→    sorted_token_pos_ptr,  # [M*top_k] int32
   264→    expert_offsets_ptr,    # [E+1] int32
   265→    out_ptr,               # [M*top_k, 2*d_expert_pad] bf16  (intermediate, gate+up interleaved)
   266→    M, E, d_hidden_half, d_expert_pad, top_k,
   267→    stride_hs_m, stride_hs_k,
   268→    stride_w1_e, stride_w1_n, stride_w1_k,
   269→    stride_ws1_e, stride_ws1_n, stride_ws1_k,
   270→    stride_out_m, stride_out_n,
   271→    BLOCK_M: tl.constexpr,   # 64 or 128 (min 16 for tl.dot_scaled)
   272→    BLOCK_N: tl.constexpr,   # 64 or 128
   273→    BLOCK_K: tl.constexpr,   # 64 (minimum for gfx950 tl.dot_scaled)
   274→):
   275→    sm_id = tl.program_id(0)
   276→    num_sms = tl.num_programs(0)
   277→
   278→    # Compute total tiles across all experts
   279→    # Each expert e has tokens_e = expert_offsets[e+1] - expert_offsets[e]
   280→    # Tiles for expert e = ceil(tokens_e / BLOCK_M) * ceil(2*d_expert_pad / BLOCK_N)
   281→    # Persistent: SM iterates over all tiles globally
   282→
   283→    tile_id = sm_id  # start tile for this SM
   284→    while tile_id < total_tiles:
   285→        # Decode tile_id → (expert_id, tile_m, tile_n)
   286→        # ... (see §6.3 for tile mapping)
   287→        e, tile_m, tile_n = decode_tile(tile_id, expert_offsets, d_expert_pad, BLOCK_M, BLOCK_N)
   288→
   289→        # Gather tokens for this tile
   290→        token_start = expert_offsets[e] + tile_m * BLOCK_M
   291→        token_end = min(token_start + BLOCK_M, expert_offsets[e+1])
   292→        m_size = token_end - token_start
   293→
   294→        # Load activation tile (gather from hidden_states)
   295→        # A: [BLOCK_M, d_hidden_half] uint8 (fp4x2) — must quantize here
   296→        # ... (activation quantization OR pass bf16 and use bf16 tl.dot)
   297→
   298→        # Load weight tile (from gate_up_weight[e])
   299→        n_start = tile_n * BLOCK_N
   300→        # B: [d_hidden_half, BLOCK_N] uint8 (fp4x2) — transposed for K-major access
   301→
   302→        # GEMM tile
   303→        acc = tl.zeros([BLOCK_M, BLOCK_N], dtype=tl.float32)
   304→        for k in range(0, d_hidden_half, BLOCK_K):
   305→            a = tl.load(...)  # [BLOCK_M, BLOCK_K] uint8
   306→            b = tl.load(...)  # [BLOCK_K, BLOCK_N] uint8
   307→            a_scale = tl.load(...)  # [BLOCK_M, BLOCK_K//16] uint8
   308→            b_scale = tl.load(...)  # [BLOCK_N, BLOCK_K//16] uint8  (N-first!)
   309→            acc = tl.dot_scaled(a, a_scale, "e2m1", b, b_scale, "e2m1", acc=acc)
   310→
   311→        # Store to intermediate buffer
   312→        out_offs = ...
   313→        tl.store(out_ptr + out_offs, acc.to(tl.bfloat16), mask=...)
   314→
   315→        tile_id += num_sms  # persistent: claim next tile
   316→```
   317→
   318→### 6.2 Stage 1.5: SiLU + multiply (fused into Stage 1 or separate)
   319→
   320→After Stage 1 GEMM, intermediate is `[M*top_k, 2*d_expert_pad]` bf16.
   321→Split into gate half and up half, apply SiLU + multiply:
   322→
   323→```triton
   324→# In-kernel fusion (preferred):
   325→gate = acc[:, :d_expert_pad]
   326→up   = acc[:, d_expert_pad:]
   327→intermediate = tl.sigmoid(gate) * gate * up  # SiLU(gate) * up
   328→```
   329→
   330→Or call `aiter.silu_and_mul(out, tmp)` as a separate kernel (adds ~2µs).
   331→
   332→### 6.3 Stage 2: down projection (down_weight, d_expert → d_hidden)
   333→
   334→Same persistent structure over `down_weight [E, d_hidden_pad, d_expert_pad//2]`.
   335→
   336→Input: intermediate (after SiLU) `[M*top_k, d_expert_pad]` bf16 or fp4x2 (if requantized).
   337→Output: `[M, d_hidden]` bf16 with topk_weight multiplication + atomic accumulation.
   338→
   339→Key difference: **must atomic-add outputs** because multiple experts contribute to each token.
   340→
   341→```triton
   342→# After computing expert output [BLOCK_M, d_hidden]:
   343→weight = tl.load(sorted_weights_ptr + sort_idx)  # [BLOCK_M] float32
   344→
   345→# Option A: atomic add (correct but may serialize)
   346→result = acc.to(tl.bfloat16) * weight[:, None]
   347→tl.atomic_add(out_ptr + orig_pos_offs, result)
   348→
   349→# Option B: write to intermediate [M*top_k, d_hidden] then reduce
   350→# (avoids atomics, adds one more pass but enables better memory patterns)
   351→```
   352→
   353→---
   354→
   355→## 7. tl.dot_scaled: MXFP4 Hardware Constraints (gfx950)
   356→
   357→**CRITICAL**: These constraints cause silent wrong results or GPU assertion failures if violated.
   358→
   359→### 7.1 Minimum tile sizes (MANDATORY)
   360→
   361→| Constraint | Value | Violation |
   362→|------------|-------|-----------|
   363→| BLOCK_M minimum | 16 | Silent wrong results (no error) |
   364→| BLOCK_K minimum | 64 (packed uint8 bytes) | `Assertion 'inputVals.size() % 4 == 0'` |
   365→| BLOCK_N | >= 32 (power of 2) | Not documented but use >= 64 for perf |
   366→
   367→```python
   368→BLOCK_M = max(16, ...)   # MANDATORY minimum 16
   369→BLOCK_K = 64             # MANDATORY minimum 64
   370→BLOCK_N = 64             # recommended
   371→```
   372→
   373→### 7.2 Scale tensor layout
   374→
   375→```python
   376→# LHS (A activation) scale: [BLOCK_M, SCALE_PER_BLOCK]
   377→SCALE_PER_BLOCK = BLOCK_K // 16   # = 4 for BLOCK_K=64
   378→
   379→# RHS (B weight) scale: [BLOCK_N, SCALE_PER_BLOCK]  -- N-FIRST, not K-first
   380→# Even though B data is [K//2, N], scale is [N, K//32] -- original N-first layout
   381→
   382→# Load scales per tile:
   383→scale_k_start = k_tile_start // 16
   384→a_scale = tl.load(A_scale_ptr + m_offs[:, None] * stride_as_m + (scale_k_start + tl.arange(0, SCALE_PER_BLOCK))[None, :])
   385→b_scale = tl.load(B_scale_ptr + n_offs[:, None] * stride_bs_n + (scale_k_start + tl.arange(0, SCALE_PER_BLOCK))[None, :])
   386→
   387→# Accumulate:
   388→acc = tl.dot_scaled(a, a_scale, "e2m1", b, b_scale, "e2m1", acc=acc)
   389→```
   390→
   391→### 7.3 CRITICAL: REDACTED KeyError blocker
   392→
   393→`KeyError: 'REDACTED'` fires in Triton's JIT when ANY tensor with dtype
   394→`torch.REDACTED` is passed to a kernel. This is a **permanent blocker on Popcorn CLI runners**.
   395→
   396→**Workaround (MANDATORY)**:
   397→```python
   398→# WRONG — Triton JIT rejects this dtype:
   399→gate_up_weight.view(torch.REDACTED)
   400→
   401→# CORRECT — view as uint8 before passing to kernel:
   402→gate_up_weight_u8 = gate_up_weight.view(torch.uint8)  # safe
   403→gate_up_weight_scale_u8 = gate_up_weight_scale.view(torch.uint8)  # safe
   404→
   405→# Inside kernel, tl.dot_scaled interprets uint8 as packed fp4 via "e2m1" format string
   406→# This works correctly — the "e2m1" string tells the hardware how to interpret the bytes
   407→```
   408→
   409→### 7.4 Activation quantization for tl.dot_scaled
   410→
   411→Activations (bf16) must be quantized to fp4x2 for Stage 1 input. Options:
   412→
   413→**Option A (fused in-kernel, preferred)**: Quantize bf16 activations inline.
   414→Use `tl.float_to_int` or custom fp4 quantization within the kernel.
   415→This is the "fused quant+GEMM" that eliminates a separate kernel launch.
   416→
   417→**Option B (pre-quantize, simpler)**: Use `aiter.ops.triton.quant.dynamic_mxfp4_quant` before kernel.
   418→```python
   419→from aiter.ops.triton.quant import dynamic_mxfp4_quant
   420→x_fp4, x_scale = dynamic_mxfp4_quant(hidden_states.contiguous())
   421→x_u8 = x_fp4.view(torch.uint8)     # [M, d_hidden//2] uint8
   422→x_scale_u8 = x_scale.view(torch.uint8)  # [M, d_hidden//32] uint8
   423→```
   424→
   425→**WARNING**: Pre-quantizing and passing pre-quantized activations into fused_moe via `a1_scale`
   426→was tested (contingency probe) and showed NO performance benefit. Option A (fused) is the
   427→novel path not yet tried.
   428→
   429→---
   430→
   431→## 8. XCD Remapping (Tile Scheduling for MI355X)
   432→
   433→MI355X has 8 XCDs (compute dies). XCD-aware tile ordering improves L2 cache reuse.
   434→
   435→### 8.1 CRITICAL BUG: Origami XCD remapping with cdiv
   436→
   437→The XCD remapping from `tritonblas.fp4_matmul` contains a **silent correctness bug**
   438→when `total_tiles % 8 != 0`. Tiles are computed twice / never reached. **DO NOT USE**.
   439→
   440→```python
   441→# BUGGY (from tritonblas) — silent wrong results when total_tiles % 8 != 0:
   442→remapped_chunk = xcd_id * tl.cdiv(num_chunks, NUM_XCDS) + chunk_in_xcd  # NON-BIJECTIVE
   443→```
   444→
   445→### 8.2 Safe alternative: group-M swizzle (RECOMMENDED)
   446→
   447→```python
   448→NUM_XCDS: tl.constexpr = 8
   449→GROUP_SIZE_M: tl.constexpr = 8  # = NUM_XCDS for XCD alignment
   450→
   451→pid = tl.program_id(0)
   452→num_pid_m = tl.cdiv(m_tiles_for_expert, BLOCK_M)
   453→num_pid_n = tl.cdiv(n_tiles_for_expert, BLOCK_N)
   454→num_pid_in_group = GROUP_SIZE_M * num_pid_n
   455→group_id = pid // num_pid_in_group
   456→first_pid_m = group_id * GROUP_SIZE_M
   457→group_size_m = min(num_pid_m - first_pid_m, GROUP_SIZE_M)
   458→pid_m = first_pid_m + (pid % group_size_m)
   459→pid_n = (pid % num_pid_in_group) // group_size_m
   460→```
   461→
   462→### 8.3 Correct XCD remapping (if XCD locality is essential)
   463→
   464→If XCD remapping is needed, use floor division (not cdiv) to ensure bijection:
   465→```python
   466→tiles_per_xcd = total_tiles // NUM_XCDS  # floor
   467→remainder = total_tiles % NUM_XCDS
   468→xcd_id = pid % NUM_XCDS
   469→chunk_in_xcd = pid // NUM_XCDS
   470→if xcd_id < remainder:
   471→    remapped = xcd_id * (tiles_per_xcd + 1) + chunk_in_xcd
   472→else:
   473→    remapped = remainder * (tiles_per_xcd + 1) + (xcd_id - remainder) * tiles_per_xcd + chunk_in_xcd
   474→```
   475→
   476→---
   477→
   478→## 9. Autotune Config Space
   479→
   480→Recommended autotune search space for MI355X gfx950:
   481→
   482→```python
   483→@triton.autotune(
   484→    configs=[
   485→        triton.Config({"BLOCK_M": bm, "BLOCK_N": bn, "BLOCK_K": bk},
   486→                      num_warps=nw, num_stages=ns)
   487→        for bm in [16, 32, 64, 128]
   488→        for bn in [64, 128, 256]
   489→        for bk in [64, 128]          # min 64 for tl.dot_scaled
   490→        for nw in [4, 8]
   491→        for ns in [2, 3, 4]
   492→    ],
   493→    key=["M", "E", "d_hidden", "d_expert"],
   494→)
   495→```
   496→
   497→**Note**: BLOCK_M=16 is the minimum for gfx950 `tl.dot_scaled`. Use `max(16, ...)` in practice.
   498→
   499→---
   500→
   501→## 10. Key Constraints and Dead Ends
   502→
   503→### HARD CONSTRAINTS (violations = crashes or wrong results)
   504→
   505→| Constraint | Value | Why |
   506→|------------|-------|-----|
   507→| `doweight_stage1=True` | NEVER | GPU crash (cktile) or 82% mismatch (CK) |
   508→| `expert_mask=bincount` | NEVER | cumsum -1 IDs → uint32(4.3B) → GPU fault |
   509→| `tl.dot_scaled` dtype | Use `"e2m1"` string, NOT torch dtype | REDACTED KeyError |
   510→| BLOCK_M | >= 16 | Silent wrong results on gfx950 |
   511→| BLOCK_K | >= 64 (packed bytes) | GPU assertion failure |
   512→| KSPLIT=4 for 32-expert | NEVER | K/4=128 below cktile minimum → ~1e27 overflow |
   513→| XCD remapping with cdiv | NEVER | Non-bijective → zeros in output |
   514→| Origami scheduling | Avoid | Only safe if total_tiles % 8 == 0 |
   515→
   516→### CONFIRMED DEAD ENDS (do NOT retry)
   517→
   518→| Approach | Status | Root Cause |
   519→|----------|--------|------------|
   520→| `fmoe_g1u1` | DEAD | NaN for 32-expert shapes; no gain for 256-expert |
   521→| Direct CK dispatch | DEAD | Replicates fused_moe overhead |
   522→| `torch.compile(fused_moe)` | DEAD | auto_functionalized_v2 on ROCm 7.1 |
   523→| `AITER_BYPASS_TUNE_CONFIG` | DEAD CODE | Competition shapes have zero CSV matches |
   524→| Custom Triton MoE (without fused permute) | 68% slower | Previous attempt didn't use fused permutation or tl.dot_scaled |
   525→| `AITER_USE_OPUS_MOE_SORTING` | No effect | Not wired into fp4x2 code path |
   526→| `block_size_M` override | GPU fault | Exceeds data bounds for small bs |
   527→
   528→### WHAT IS NOVEL (not previously attempted)
   529→
   530→1. **Fused token permutation** — eliminate `moe_sorting_fwd` as separate kernel
   531→2. **`tl.dot_scaled` with `"e2m1"` string** (not dtype) — hardware MXFP4 GEMM
   532→3. **Persistent tiles over ALL experts** — single kernel launch for all E experts
   533→4. **Fused SiLU in Stage 1** — no separate `silu_and_mul` call
   534→5. **Inline activation quantization** — fuse fp4 quant into Stage 1 GEMM
   535→
   536→---
   537→
   538→## 11. Recommended Implementation Path
   539→
   540→### Phase 1: Correctness (bf16 GEMM, no fused permute)
   541→
   542→Start with bf16 (not fp4) to validate the persistent tile loop and permutation logic:
   543→1. Sort tokens per expert in Python (§5.1)
   544→2. Implement Stage 1 kernel: persistent tiles, gather from sorted_token_pos, bf16 matmul
   545→3. Fuse SiLU+multiply
   546→4. Implement Stage 2: persistent tiles, atomic-add with topk_weight
   547→5. Verify correctness vs reference (rtol=2e-2, atol=2e-2)
   548→
   549→### Phase 2: MXFP4 acceleration
   550→
   551→Replace bf16 matmul with `tl.dot_scaled`:
   552→1. View fp4x2 weights as uint8 before kernel call
   553→2. Pre-quantize activations via `dynamic_mxfp4_quant` → uint8 view
   554→3. Pass scales as uint8, load per-tile with N-first layout for B-scale
   555→4. Use BLOCK_K=64 minimum
   556→
   557→### Phase 3: Performance tuning
   558→
   559→1. Autotune BLOCK_M/N/K
   560→2. Try inline activation quantization (fuse Stage 1 quant)
   561→3. Try group-M swizzle scheduling
   562→4. Profile with `torch.cuda.nvtx` to identify bottleneck
   563→
   564→### Phase 4: Risk mitigation fallback
   565→
   566→If custom Triton is slower than ~155µs, fall back to `aiter.fused_moe` as a baseline.
   567→The submission structure should support:
   568→```python
   569→if USE_CUSTOM_KERNEL:
   570→    return custom_triton_moe(...)
   571→else:
   572→    return fm(hs, w1sh, w2sh, ...)  # aiter fallback
   573→```
   574→
   575→---
   576→
   577→## 12. Expected Performance Analysis
   578→
   579→| Source | Latency | Notes |
   580→|--------|---------|-------|
   581→| aiter fused_moe (current) | ~155µs geomean | Baseline |
   582→| Leader | ~145µs | Target |
   583→| Custom Triton (previous, no fused permute) | ~250µs | 68% slower — dead end |
   584→| Custom Triton (with fused permute + tl.dot_scaled) | ??? | Novel path |
   585→
   586→Expected savings from the novel approach:
   587→- Fused permutation: -5-10µs (eliminates `moe_sorting_fwd` kernel)
   588→- Persistent scheduling: -2-5µs (fewer kernel launches)
   589→- Native MXFP4 via tl.dot_scaled: hardware-accelerated, potentially faster for small tiles
   590→
   591→Risk: Previous custom Triton was 68% slower than CK ASM. The new approach differs in:
   592→(a) fused permutation (not tried before), (b) tl.dot_scaled (not tried before),
   593→(c) persistent-tile scheduling (not tried before). Combined effect is unknown.
   594→
   595→---
   596→
   597→## 13. Code Skeleton (Implementation Starting Point)
   598→
   599→```python
   600→# research/challenges/luma_amd_speedrun/kernels/moe-mxfp4/submission.py
   601→import os, torch, triton, triton.language as tl
   602→from task import input_t, output_t
   603→from aiter.ops.triton.quant import dynamic_mxfp4_quant
   604→
   605→os.environ["AITER_USE_NT"] = "1"
   606→
   607→# ── Stage 1 kernel: hidden × gate_up_weight → intermediate (gate+up) ──────────
   608→@triton.autotune(configs=[...], key=["M", "E", "d_hidden", "d_expert"])
   609→@triton.jit
   610→def _moe_stage1_kernel(
   611→    hidden_ptr, w1_ptr, w1_scale_ptr,
   612→    sorted_pos_ptr, expert_offsets_ptr,
   613→    out_ptr,
   614→    M, E: tl.constexpr, d_hidden_half, d_expert_pad,
   615→    # strides...
   616→    BLOCK_M: tl.constexpr, BLOCK_N: tl.constexpr, BLOCK_K: tl.constexpr,
   617→):
   618→    SCALE_PER_BLOCK: tl.constexpr = BLOCK_K // 16
   619→    sm_id = tl.program_id(0)
   620→    num_sms = tl.num_programs(0)
   621→    # persistent tile loop...
   622→
   623→# ── Stage 2 kernel: intermediate × down_weight → output (with atomic add) ─────
   624→@triton.autotune(configs=[...], key=["M", "E", "d_hidden", "d_expert"])
   625→@triton.jit
   626→def _moe_stage2_kernel(
   627→    intermediate_ptr, w2_ptr, w2_scale_ptr,
   628→    sorted_pos_ptr, sorted_weights_ptr, expert_offsets_ptr,
   629→    out_ptr,
   630→    M, E: tl.constexpr, d_expert_pad, d_hidden,
   631→    # strides...
   632→    BLOCK_M: tl.constexpr, BLOCK_N: tl.constexpr, BLOCK_K: tl.constexpr,
   633→):
   634→    SCALE_PER_BLOCK: tl.constexpr = BLOCK_K // 16
   635→    sm_id = tl.program_id(0)
   636→    # persistent tile loop with atomic_add...
   637→
   638→def custom_kernel(data: input_t) -> output_t:
   639→    (hs, w1, w2, w1s, w2s, w1sh, w2sh, w1ssh, w2ssh, tw, ti, cfg) = data
   640→
   641→    M, d_hidden = hs.shape
   642→    E = w1.shape[0]
   643→    d_expert_pad = cfg["d_expert_pad"]
   644→    top_k = ti.shape[1]
   645→
   646→    # Pre-sort tokens by expert (replaces moe_sorting_fwd)
   647→    flat_ids = ti.view(-1).long()
   648→    flat_w = tw.view(-1)
   649→    sort_order = torch.argsort(flat_ids)
   650→    sorted_experts = flat_ids[sort_order].to(torch.int32)
   651→    sorted_pos = (sort_order // top_k).to(torch.int32)  # original token index
   652→    sorted_weights = flat_w[sort_order]
   653→    tokens_per_expert = torch.bincount(ti.view(-1).long(), minlength=E).to(torch.int32)
   654→    expert_offsets = torch.zeros(E+1, dtype=torch.int32, device=hs.device)
   655→    expert_offsets[1:] = tokens_per_expert.cumsum(0)
   656→
   657→    # Pre-quantize activations (Option B — simplest)
   658→    # For Option A (in-kernel quant), skip this step
   659→    x_fp4, x_scale = dynamic_mxfp4_quant(hs.contiguous())
   660→    x_u8 = x_fp4.view(torch.uint8)
   661→    x_scale_u8 = x_scale.view(torch.uint8)
   662→
   663→    # View weights as uint8 (required — REDACTED KeyError workaround)
   664→    w1_u8 = w1.view(torch.uint8)       # [E, 2*d_expert_pad, d_hidden_pad//2]
   665→    w1s_u8 = w1s.view(torch.uint8)     # [E, 2*d_expert_pad, scale_K]
   666→    w2_u8 = w2.view(torch.uint8)       # [E, d_hidden_pad, d_expert_pad//2]
   667→    w2s_u8 = w2s.view(torch.uint8)     # [E, d_hidden_pad, scale_K]
   668→
   669→    # Allocate intermediates
   670→    intermediate = torch.zeros(M * top_k, 2 * d_expert_pad, dtype=torch.bfloat16, device=hs.device)
   671→    output = torch.zeros(M, d_hidden, dtype=torch.bfloat16, device=hs.device)
   672→
   673→    # Launch Stage 1
   674→    num_sms = torch.cuda.get_device_properties(0).multi_processor_count
   675→    _moe_stage1_kernel[(num_sms,)](
   676→        x_u8, w1_u8, w1s_u8,
   677→        sorted_pos, expert_offsets,
   678→        intermediate,
   679→        M, E, ...
   680→    )
   681→
   682→    # Apply SiLU + multiply (fuse into stage1 or separate)
   683→    gate = intermediate[:, :d_expert_pad]
   684→    up = intermediate[:, d_expert_pad:]
   685→    intermediate_silu = torch.nn.functional.silu(gate) * up  # [M*top_k, d_expert_pad]
   686→
   687→    # Re-quantize for Stage 2 (or keep bf16)
   688→    # ...
   689→
   690→    # Launch Stage 2
   691→    _moe_stage2_kernel[(num_sms,)](
   692→        intermediate_silu, w2_u8, w2s_u8,
   693→        sorted_pos, sorted_weights, expert_offsets,
   694→        output,
   695→        M, E, ...
   696→    )
   697→
   698→    return output
   699→```
   700→

All checks pass. Here is a summary of what was implemented.

---

**File written:** `/home/mike-anderson/dev/cohezion/research/challenges/luma_amd_speedrun/kernels/moe-mxfp4/submission_custom_triton_v2.py`

**What was implemented (Phase 1 — bf16 GEMM correctness):**

**Python-side token sorting (`_sort_tokens_by_expert`, §5.1)**
- `torch.argsort(flat_ids, stable=True)` sorts all M×top_k token-expert pairs by expert ID
- `sort_order // top_k` recovers original token row index → `sorted_token_pos [M*top_k]`
- `torch.bincount` + `cumsum` builds `expert_offsets [E+1]` prefix-sum array
- Replaces `moe_sorting_fwd` without a separate kernel launch

**Weight dequantization (`_dequant_all_experts_bf16`)**
- Uses `fp4_utils.mxfp4_to_f32` + `fp4_utils.e8m0_to_f32` (same as reference)
- Converts fp4x2 weights → bf16 once before kernel launch (Phase 1 strategy)
- Phase 2 will replace this with `tl.dot_scaled("e2m1")` and uint8-viewed fp4x2

**Stage 1 Triton kernel (`_moe_stage1_kernel`)**
- Persistent-tile loop: SM claims tiles by global `tile_id`, strides by `num_sms`
- Linear scan over expert_offsets to decode `tile_id → (expert, tile_m, tile_n)`
- Gather: loads `orig_pos` from `sorted_token_pos`, gathers rows from `hidden_states`
- bf16 GEMM via `tl.dot(a, tl.trans(b), input_precision="ieee")` — no fp4 needed yet
- Stores result to `intermediate [M*top_k, 2*d_expert_pad]`

**SiLU + multiply** — applied in PyTorch between stages: `F.silu(gate) * up`

**Stage 2 Triton kernel (`_moe_stage2_kernel`)**
- Same persistent-tile structure over `down_weight [E, d_hidden_pad, d_expert_pad]`
- Reads `sorted_weights` for topk scaling
- Uses `tl.atomic_add` per row to accumulate into `output [M, d_hidden]` — required because multiple experts write to the same token row

**Key constraints respected:**
- `BLOCK_M = 32` (above the mandatory 16 minimum for gfx950)
- `BLOCK_K = 64` (minimum for gfx950 dot operations)
- No XCD remapping with `cdiv` (avoids the non-bijective bug from §8.1)
- `USE_CUSTOM_TRITON=0` falls back to aiter `fused_moe` with pre-shuffled weights

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**Sprint retrospective — what worked and what to improve**:

**Worked well:**
- Researcher → Writer pipeline: spec-first development produced a 644-line kernel in one shot
- Parallel research + evolution: kernel-researcher analyzed code while tree-evolver ran LLM cycles
- Agent definitions saved for reuse — next sprint starts instantly

**Needs improvement:**
- Tree-evolver's recommendation is spot-on: `MAX_TOKENS=4096` is way too high for the world model role. It only needs ~100 tokens of JSON output, but the model generates up to 4096 tokens of reasoning before the JSON. Reducing to 512 would cut inference time by ~8x.
- CPU-only Ollama is fundamentally too slow for interactive use. The overnight use case is fine.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

{
  "command": "python3 -c \"\nimport json, time\nfrom urllib.request import Request, urlopen\n\nschema = {\n    'type': 'object',\n    'properties': {\n        'insert': {'type': 'array', 'items': {'type': 'object', 'properties': {'parent_id': {'type': 'string'}, 'strategy': {'type': 'string'}, 'priority': {'type': 'number'}}, 'required': ['strategy', 'priority']}},\n        'update': {'type': 'object'},\n        'prune': {'type': 'array', 'items': {'type': 'string'}}\n    },\n    'required': ['insert', 'update', 'prune']\n}\n\nprompt = '''GPU MoE kernel optimization. KSPLIT=4 for sparse 256-expert shapes gave 148us.\nThe 32-expert shapes are the bottleneck at 180us. Current tree has nodes for KSPLIT sweep 1-6\nand direct CK dispatch. Suggest 1-2 new strategies as JSON with insert/update/prune.'''\n\nfor m in ['qwen3.5:cloud', 'qwen3-coder-next:cloud']:\n    req = Request('http://localhost:11434/api/generate',\n        data=json.dumps({'model':m,'prompt':prompt,'stream':True,'format':schema,'options':{'num_predict':200}}).encode(),\n        headers={'Content-Type':'application/json'}, method='POST')\n    tokens = []\n    t0 = time.time()\n    with urlopen(req, timeout=60) as r:\n        for line in r:\n            if not line.strip(): continue\n            d = json.loads(line)\n            if d.get('response'): tokens.append(d['response'])\n            if d.get('done'): break\n    text = ''.join(tokens).strip()\n    elapsed = time.time()-t0\n    print(f'=== {m} ({elapsed:.1f}s, {len(tokens)} tokens) ===')\n    print(text[:500])\n    # Validate JSON\n    try:\n        parsed = json.loads(text)\n        print(f'  Valid JSON: insert={len(parsed.get(\\\"insert\\\",[]))} update={len(parsed.get(\\\"update\\\",{}))} prune={len(parsed.get(\\\"prune\\\",[]))}')\n    except: print('  INVALID JSON')\n    print()\n\"",

  "timeout": 120000
}
